# RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback

Xiaoying Zhang zhangxycuhk@gmail.comShanghai AI Lab    Zichen
LiuNational University of Singapore    Yipeng ZhangIndependent
Researcher    Xia HuShanghai AI Lab    Wenqi Shao^(†)Shanghai AI Lab   
[https://github.com/zhangxy-2019/RetroAgent](https://github.com/zhangxy-2019/RetroAgent)

###### Abstract

Standard reinforcement learning (RL) for large language model (LLM)
agents primarily optimizes extrinsic task rewards, often favoring
isolated task completion over continual adaptation. This paradigm can
cause premature convergence to suboptimal policies and leaves useful
experience only implicitly encoded in model parameters, limiting its
retrieval and reuse for future decisions. We introduce RetroAgent, an
online RL framework that trains agents to master interactive
environments not merely by solving tasks, but by evolving across
episodes. Inspired by human retrospective self-improvement, RetroAgent
augments extrinsic rewards with hindsight-generated dual intrinsic
feedback: $`(\textup{\it i})`$ *Intrinsic Numerical Feedback*, which
rewards beneficial exploration by measuring incremental subtask progress
relative to prior attempts; and $`(\textup{\it ii})`$ *Intrinsic
Language Feedback*, which distills successes and failures into reusable
textual lessons for explicit experience reuse. To leverage these lessons
effectively, we propose _Similarity & Utility-Aware Upper Confidence
Bound_ (SimUtil-UCB), a retrieval strategy that balances semantic
relevance, historical utility, and exploration. Across four challenging
agentic benchmarks, RetroAgent achieves new state-of-the-art
performance, outperforming GRPO by $`+18.3\%`$ on ALFWorld, $`+15.4\%`$
on WebShop, $`+27.1\%`$ on Sokoban, and $`+8.9\%`$ on MineSweeper, while
demonstrating strong test-time adaptation and out-of-distribution
generalization.

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/retroagent_intro.drawio.png)

Figure 1: (a) Overview of RetroAgent. After each episode, the agent
reflects on its trajectory to generate dual intrinsic feedback,
promoting exploration and facilitating experiential learning.
(b) Initialized from Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, RetroAgent substantially
outperforms the GRPO baseline and achieves new SOTA across four agentic
benchmarks.

## 1 Introduction

Reinforcement learning (RL) ([Sutton et al., 1998](#bib.bib30)) has
become a central paradigm for training large language model (LLM) agents
to act in complex interactive environments through direct
experience ([Ouyang et al., 2022](#bib.bib54); [Zhang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib49); [Comanici et al., 2025](#bib.bib45); [Singh et al.,
2025](#bib.bib61); [Liu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib50)). However, most
existing RL frameworks primarily optimize extrinsic task-success
rewards, favoring _one-off task solving_ over _continuous adaptation_
across interactions ([Abel et al., 2023](#bib.bib51); [Weng,
2026](#bib.bib58); [Rafiee and Sutton, 2026](#bib.bib55)). For example,
in embodied-agent tasks, training often terminates once an agent finds a
successful action sequence. Such success-driven optimization has two
limitations. First, agents may over-exploit early successes and
prematurely converge to suboptimal policies instead of exploring
alternatives ([Kirk et al., 2024](#bib.bib59)). Second, useful
experience is usually stored only implicitly in model parameters, making
prior interactions difficult to retrieve, inspect, and reuse for later
policy improvement ([Lin, 1992](#bib.bib52); [Graves et al.,
2014](#bib.bib53)). This can slow learning and weaken generalization,
especially when relevant experience is sparse or
context-dependent ([Goyal et al., 2022](#bib.bib60)).

Existing work addresses these limitations in two largely separate ways.
Exploration-oriented methods broaden search through, for example,
meta-RL with cross-episode training ([Jiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib3);
[Beck et al., 2025](#bib.bib72)) or uncertainty-modulated rewards under
sparse feedback ([Wang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib77)). Yet they do not
explicitly preserve reusable experience, limiting the role of past
interactions in sustained policy evolution. Conversely, memory-augmented
methods store raw trajectories ([Goyal et al., 2022](#bib.bib60); [Wu et
al., 2025](#bib.bib21); [Liu et al., 2026b](#bib.bib48)) or distilled
skills, rules, and lessons ([Anthropic, 2025](#bib.bib46); [Wang et al.,
2025c](#bib.bib44); [Liu et al., 2026b](#bib.bib48); [Xia et al.,
2026](#bib.bib22)). However, memory is typically used as _passive
context augmentation_: retrieval is governed by fixed similarity
metrics, regardless of downstream utility, and remains decoupled from
policy optimization. As a result, agents may become brittle when memory
is noisy or unavailable, while over-reliance on retrieved experience can
suppress further exploration.

These observations motivate a central question: _Can an agent couple
exploration with explicit experience reuse so that experience guides
policy evolution and is gradually internalized, rather than being
persistently supplied by an external memory buffer?_ More broadly, can
agents exploit intrinsic signals from their own trajectories to
evolve ^(\*)^(\*) \* In this paper, we use _evolution_ to denote the
joint process of task solving and continuous adaptation ([Hinton et al.,
1987](#bib.bib57); [Lehman et al., 2023](#bib.bib56)). under sparse
external supervision?

To answer this question, we introduce RetroAgent
(Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
an online RL framework inspired by human retrospective
reflection ([Lyons and Zelazo, 2011](#bib.bib28); [Liu and van der
Schaar, 2025](#bib.bib76)). RetroAgent trains agents to master complex
interactive environments not merely by solving tasks, but by evolving
across episodes under both extrinsic task-success rewards and
retrospective dual intrinsic feedback. After each episode, the agent
analyzes its trajectory to diagnose successes and failures, identify
useful progress, and generate two complementary feedback signals.
Intrinsic Numerical Feedback rewards exploratory behavior that reflects
capability improvement. Since real-time capability evolution is hard to
measure directly, we approximate it by incremental subtask progress
relative to prior attempts, such as locating a target item even when the
final purchase fails. The resulting scalar reward reinforces beneficial
exploration and mitigates premature convergence. Intrinsic Language
Feedback distills actionable lessons from successful and failed
trajectories into an explicit memory buffer for future decision-making.
To retrieve useful lessons, we propose _Similarity & Utility-Aware Upper
Confidence Bound_ (SimUtil-UCB), which combines semantic relevance with
historical utility and uses Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) ([Auer et al.,
2002](#bib.bib47)) to balance exploiting high-utility lessons with
exploring under-used ones.

We study two variants of RetroAgent: $`(\textup{\it i})`$ an
_in-context_ self-reflection mechanism, and $`(\textup{\it ii})`$ an
_RL-trained_ self-reflection mechanism whose reflective capability is
jointly optimized with the decision policy. RetroAgent is compatible
with various RL algorithms; in our implementation, we optimize the
decision policy with GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024b](#bib.bib25)) and the
self-reflection policy with REINFORCE ([Williams, 1992](#bib.bib24)). We
evaluate RetroAgent using Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct ([Qwen et al.,
2025](#bib.bib70)) and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ([Grattafiori et al.,
2024](#bib.bib69)) on four agentic benchmarks: ALFWorld ([Shridhar et
al., 2021](#bib.bib36)), WebShop ([Yao et al., 2022b](#bib.bib35)),
Sokoban ([Racanière et al., 2017](#bib.bib33)), and MineSweeper ([Li et
al., 2024](#bib.bib34)). Across all environments, RetroAgent
consistently outperforms prior RL fine-tuning, memory-augmented RL,
exploration-guided RL, and meta-RL baselines, improving SOTA success
rates by approximately $`+10\%`$ on WebShop and $`+16\%`$ on Sokoban,
while exhibiting strong test-time adaptation and out-of-distribution
generalization.

Our contributions are four-fold: $`(\textup{\it i})`$ We introduce
RetroAgent, an online RL framework with hindsight self-reflection for
continuous policy evolution in interactive environments.
$`(\textup{\it ii})`$ We design dual intrinsic feedback to promote
beneficial exploration and efficient experiential learning.
$`(\textup{\it iii})`$ We propose SimUtil-UCB, a retrieval strategy that
balances semantic similarity, historical utility, and exploration when
leveraging accumulated lessons. $`(\textup{\it iv})`$ We demonstrate
across four agentic benchmarks that RetroAgent substantially outperforms
strong baselines and achieves new SOTA results in both in-distribution
and out-of-distribution settings.

## 2 Related Work

#### LLMs as Decision-Making Agents.

The reasoning capabilities of LLMs have driven their deployment as
autonomous decision-making agents. An initial line of research prompts
frozen LLMs: ReAct ([Yao et al., 2022c](#bib.bib4)), Reflexion ([Shinn
et al., 2023](#bib.bib6)), and related methods ([Park et al.,
2023](#bib.bib7); [Wang et al., 2024a](#bib.bib8)) leverage in-context
examples, structured prompts, memory retrieval ([Wang et al.,
2024b](#bib.bib9)), and external tools ([Schick et al.,
2023](#bib.bib10); [Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib64); [Zhang et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib65)) to tackle complex tasks. However, these approaches
are inherently bounded by the capabilities of the underlying foundation
model. This ceiling has motivated a second line of work that trains LLM
agents directly—through supervised fine-tuning ([Tajwar et al.,
2025](#bib.bib2); [Xi et al., 2025](#bib.bib63)) or RL ([Song et al.,
2024](#bib.bib62); [Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib43); [Feng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib5); [Jiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib3))—enabling them to
improve from environmental interactions rather than relying on static
prompts or handcrafted workflows.

#### Reinforcement Learning for LLM Agents.

RL has become a central paradigm for training agents in multi-turn,
dynamic environments ([Wang et al., 2025d](#bib.bib73); [Putta et al.,
2025](#bib.bib11); [Liu et al., 2025a](#bib.bib1); [Liu et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib50)). ArCHer ([Zhou et al., 2024](#bib.bib42)) employs
hierarchical value functions for WebShop ([Yao et al.,
2022a](#bib.bib41)), while LOOP ([Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib66))
integrates PPO ([Schulman et al., 2017](#bib.bib14)) with Leave-One-Out
advantage estimation for long-horizon tasks in AppWorld ([Trivedi et
al., 2024](#bib.bib13)). Group-based RL methods have further refined
credit assignment: building on GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024a](#bib.bib12)),
GiGPO ([Feng et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)) introduces two-level advantage
estimation, while other works investigate turn-level reward
shaping ([Wei et al., 2025](#bib.bib74)) and stepwise progress
attribution ([Wang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib78)). Meta-RL ([Beck et al.,
2025](#bib.bib72)) offers a complementary perspective; notably,
LAMER ([Jiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)) uses cross-episode training to
enable active test-time exploration. However, these methods optimize
primarily against extrinsic environmental feedback, and recent analyses
argue that genuine self-improvement requires intrinsic signals beyond
sparse task rewards ([Liu and van der Schaar, 2025](#bib.bib76)).
Although prior works have explored intrinsic motivation ([Gao et al.,
2025](#bib.bib75)) or entropy-modulated policies ([Wang et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib77)), RetroAgent takes a fundamentally different path: a
hindsight self-reflection mechanism produces dual intrinsic feedback,
shifting the objective from isolated problem-solving toward continuous
adaptation.

#### Learning from Experience through Retrospection.

A growing body of work moves beyond scalar rewards by leveraging verbal
feedback and retrospective memory for agent self-improvement. Early
approaches ([Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib6); [Madaan et al.,
2023](#bib.bib39); [Yao et al., 2024](#bib.bib38)) generate
natural-language critiques or lessons from interactions, iteratively
refining same-task performance via in-context learning. Subsequent work
internalizes such feedback into model parameters: [Jiang et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib3) use reflections to guide cross-episode adaptation
within a meta-RL framework, while [Zhang et al. (2025c)](#bib.bib32);
[Hübotter et al. (2026)](#bib.bib18) refine failed trajectories into
high-quality data for policy optimization through RL or distillation. A
complementary direction adopts memory-based architectures ([Goyal et
al., 2022](#bib.bib60); [Wu et al., 2025](#bib.bib21); [Wang et al.,
2025c](#bib.bib44); [Zhang et al., 2026](#bib.bib23); [Zhou et al.,
2025](#bib.bib31); [Fang et al., 2026](#bib.bib19); [Liu et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib48)) that store trajectories, lessons, or skills ([Xia et
al., 2026](#bib.bib22)) in a retrieval buffer to assist similar future
tasks in context. RetroAgent advances this paradigm along a new axis:
the agent reflects on its trajectories to produce both intrinsic
numerical rewards that guide exploration and intrinsic language feedback
that facilitates exploiting past experiences, with these dual signals
jointly driving policy optimization.

## 3 RetroAgent

We introduce RetroAgent
(Figure [2](#S3.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
an online reinforcement learning framework that employs hindsight
self-reflection to improve exploration and experiential learning under
sparse extrinsic feedback.
Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 General Overview ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
formalizes the problem and overviews the self-reflection mechanism.
Next, we detail our strategies for utilizing intrinsic feedback:
Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
introduces numerical feedback to promote exploration, while
Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
explains how language feedback enables the exploitation of past
experience. Finally,
Section [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Policy Optimization with Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
establishes the policy optimization objectives for both RetroAgent
variants.

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/retroagent_method.drawio.png)

Figure 2: Overview of the RetroAgent framework. After each episode, a
self-reflection mechanism analyzes the trajectory to produce two forms
of intrinsic feedback: $`(\textup{\it i})`$ *Intrinsic Numerical
Feedback*, which quantifies incremental subtask completion relative to
prior attempts, rewarding promising exploratory behaviors that may not
yet yield task success; and $`(\textup{\it ii})`$ *Intrinsic Language
Feedback*, which distills actionable lessons from past successes and
failures into a memory buffer, retrieved via the proposed SimUtil-UCB
strategy to effectively leverage accumulated experiences on similar
tasks.

### 3.1 General Overview

#### Problem Formulation.

We model the LLM agent’s multi-turn interaction with its environment as
a Markov Decision Process (MDP) ([Sutton et al., 1998](#bib.bib30)),
defined by $`\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P,R,\gamma)`$, where
$`\mathcal{S}`$ is the state space, $`\mathcal{A}`$ the action space,
$`P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})`$ the environment’s transition dynamics,
$`R(s_{t},a_{t})`$ the reward function, and $`\gamma\in[0,1]`$ the
discount factor. At each step $`t=0,\dots,T{-}1`$, the agent observes
state $`s_{t}\in\mathcal{S}`$ and samples action $`a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}`$
from its policy $`\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s_{t})`$. In the LLM agent
setting, the state is the concatenation of all preceding observations
and actions: $`s_{t}=(o_{0},a_{0},\dots,a_{t-1},o_{t})`$. Executing
$`a_{t}`$ yields reward $`r_{t+1}=R(s_{t},a_{t})`$ and successor state
$`s_{t+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{t},a_{t})`$, producing a trajectory
$`\tau=(s_{0},a_{0},r_{1},\dots,s_{T-1},a_{T-1},r_{T})`$. With purely
extrinsic rewards $`r_{t+1}=r_{t+1}^{\text{ext}}`$, the standard
objective is to maximize the expected discounted return:

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathcal{J}_{\text{Standard}}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\times P}\!\left[\,G_{0}\,\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\times P}\!\left[\,\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\,r_{t+1}^{\text{ext}}\right],
``` |  | (1) |

where $`x=o_{0}`$ is the task instruction drawn from the training
set $`\mathcal{D}`$, and $`\tau\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\times P`$
denotes that trajectories are generated jointly by the policy and the
environment dynamics. In practice, extrinsic rewards are sparse: a
non-zero terminal reward $`R^{\text{ext}}`$ is provided only when the
episode ends, either upon successful task completion or upon exceeding
the allowed number of steps. To simplify credit assignment, we
redistribute this terminal reward uniformly across all steps, setting
$`r_{t+1}^{\text{ext}}=R^{\text{ext}}`$ for every $`t`$.

RetroAgent augments this objective with intrinsic feedback from a
hindsight self-reflection mechanism. An intrinsic reward
$`R^{\text{int}}`$
(Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"))
is likewise assigned uniformly to every step, yielding the composite
objective:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{J}_{\text{RetroAgent}}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\Pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\,\mathcal{M})\times P}\left[\,\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\left(R^{\text{ext}}+R^{\text{int}}\right)\right],
``` |  | (2) |

where $`\Pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\,\mathcal{M})`$ denotes a mixture
distribution over trajectories induced by two policies: the base policy
$`\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)`$ and a memory-augmented policy
$`\pi_{\theta}\!\bigl(\cdot\mid f_{\text{memory}}(x,\,\mathcal{M})\bigr)`$.
Here, $`f_{\text{memory}}(x,\mathcal{M})`$ represents the proposed
SimUtil-UCB retrieval strategy
(Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
which selects a memory instance that is both relevant and useful from
the memory buffer $`\mathcal{M}`$ (which grows over time) to augment the
task instruction $`x`$.

#### Hindsight Self-Reflection.

At its core, RetroAgent incorporates a hindsight self-reflection
mechanism for efficient experiential learning. At the conclusion of each
episode, the agent evaluates its trajectory via a reflection function
$`z=f_{\text{reflect}}(\tau)`$, leveraging in-context learning ([Wei et
al., 2022](#bib.bib37)).^(†)^(†) † For notational simplicity, we reuse
$`\tau`$ to denote the agent–environment interaction history, consisting
of interleaved observations and actions. This function produces a
reflection tuple $`z=(\phi_{(x,\tau)},c,l)`$ comprising three
components: $`(\textup{\it i})`$ a scalar potential score
$`\phi_{(x,\tau)}\in[0,1]`$ estimating the subtask completion rate, from
which the intrinsic numerical reward $`R^{\text{int}}`$ is derived
(Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"));
$`(\textup{\it ii})`$ a binary success prediction
$`c\in\{\text{success},\text{failure}\}`$; and $`(\textup{\it iii})`$ a
natural-language retrospective lesson $`l`$ distilled from the
trajectory. The lesson $`l`$, encapsulated within a memory entry $`m`$,
is stored in a memory buffer $`\mathcal{M}`$ and subsequently retrieved
via $`f_{\text{memory}}(x,\,\mathcal{M})`$ to provide intrinsic language
feedback
(Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).

The central challenge of this mechanism lies in eliciting high-quality
intrinsic feedback. To this end, we propose two variants: an
*in-context* variant and an *RL-trained* variant.

In-Context Variant. We employ pairwise induction by augmenting the
reflection function with two additional inputs: $`(\text{i})`$ a binary
outcome indicator
$`I^{\text{ext}}\in\{\text{success},\text{failure}\}`$, and
$`(\text{ii})`$ a contrastive reference trajectory $`\tau_{\text{ref}}`$
collected from an earlier training step whose outcome differs from that
of the current episode. Contrasting successful and failed trajectories
enables the model to more precisely isolate behavioral strengths and
deficiencies, yielding higher-quality potential scores and lessons ([Lee
et al., 2023](#bib.bib16)). The resulting reflection function takes the
form
$`z=f_{\text{reflect}}(\tau_{\text{ref}},\,I^{\text{ext}},\,\tau)`$.

RL-Trained Variant. In this variant, the agent is jointly optimized so
that its self-reflection capability co-evolves with its decision-making
policy. We introduce a reflection reward $`R^{\text{reflect}}`$ that
quantifies the accuracy of the agent’s self-assessment:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R^{\text{reflect}}:=R^{\text{ext},(i)}\cdot\mathbf{1}\{c=I^{\mathrm{ext}}\},
``` |  | (3) |

where $`\mathbf{1}\{\cdot\}`$ is the indicator function and $`c`$ is the
success prediction produced by the reflection. Scaling by
$`R^{\text{ext}}`$ aligns the magnitude of the reflection reward with
that of the extrinsic signal.^(‡)^(‡) ‡ Alternative reward-scaling
strategies are possible but are left for future work. Let
$`\varphi_{\theta}`$ denote the reflection policy, which generates the
reflection tuple $`z=(\phi_{(x,\tau)},\,c,\,l)`$ conditioned on the
trajectory $`\tau`$. The composite training objective generalizes
Equation [2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ Problem Formulation. ‣ 3.1 General Overview ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
by incorporating a self-reflection term:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{J}_{\text{RetroAgent}}(\theta)=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\Pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x,\,\mathcal{M})\times P}\left[\,\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\left(R^{\text{ext}}+R^{\text{int}}\right)\right]}_{\text{Decision-Making}}\;+\;\underbrace{\lambda_{\text{reflect}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\varphi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid\tau)}\left[\,R^{\text{reflect}}\right]}_{\text{Self-Reflection}},
``` |  | (4) |

where $`\lambda_{\text{reflect}}\geq 0`$ is a coefficient controlling
the relative weight of the self-reflection objective;
Equation [2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ Problem Formulation. ‣ 3.1 General Overview ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
is recovered when $`\lambda_{\text{reflect}}=0`$. Prompt templates for
both variants are provided in
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Task Prompts ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
and optimization details are discussed in
Section [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Policy Optimization with Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

### 3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration

Sparse terminal rewards do not distinguish uninformative failures from
trajectories that make meaningful partial progress. This limitation is
especially severe for LLM agents, whose action spaces are large and
whose useful intermediate reasoning or tool-use behaviors may not
immediately produce task success. To provide a directed exploration
signal, RetroAgent converts the reflected potential score
$`\phi_{(x,\tau)}`$ into an intrinsic *capability-evolution* reward.
This reward assigns intrinsic credit only to trajectories whose
reflected progress exceeds the best group-level performance previously
observed for the same task.

For each task $`x`$, we maintain a historical baseline
$`\Phi_{x}^{(k)}`$, where $`\Phi_{x}^{(0)}=0`$. The baseline records the
maximum group-mean extrinsic success rate observed before iteration
$`k`$. After collecting $`N`$ rollouts for task $`x`$ at iteration
$`k`$, we compute
$`\bar{I}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{k}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}I_{k}^{\mathrm{ext},(j)},`$
where $`I_{k}^{\mathrm{ext},(j)}\in\{0,1\}`$ denotes the extrinsic
success indicator of the $`j`$-th rollout. The baseline is then updated
by

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Phi_{x}^{(k+1)}=\max\left\{\Phi_{x}^{(k)},\bar{I}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{k}(x)\right\}.
``` |  | (5) |

Given a trajectory $`\tau`$ with reflected potential score
$`\phi_{(x,\tau),k}`$, its intrinsic reward is defined as the rectified
gain over the current baseline:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)=\left[\phi_{(x,\tau),k}-\Phi_{x}^{(k)}\right]_{+},\qquad[a]_{+}\coloneqq\max\{a,0\}.
``` |  | (6) |

The trajectory-level reward used for policy optimization is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau)=R^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tau)+R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau).
``` |  |

When the iteration is clear from context, we omit the superscript $`k`$.

This reward has two useful properties. First, it is
*progress-sensitive*: even failed trajectories may receive positive
intrinsic reward if their reflected potential exceeds the historical
baseline. Second, it is *self-normalizing*: since $`\Phi_{x}^{(k)}`$ is
monotonically non-decreasing, intrinsic credit is assigned only to
trajectories that improve upon the agent’s current capability threshold.
Thus, the reward encourages capability improvement rather than
undirected novelty or repeated partial behaviors.

#### Theoretical Justification.

We next formalize why the capability-evolution reward promotes directed
exploration. For a task $`x`$ and policy class $`\Pi`$, define the
extrinsic objective of policy $`\pi\in\Pi`$ as
$`J_{\mathrm{ext}}(\pi;x)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)\times P}\left[R^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tau)\right],`$
and define the augmented objective at iteration $`k`$ as
$`J_{\mathrm{aug}}(\pi;x)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)\times P}\left[R^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tau)+R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\right].`$

###### Definition 1 (Valuable exploratory trajectory).

At iteration $`k`$, a trajectory $`\tau`$ is $`\delta`$-valuable for
task $`x`$ if its reflected potential exceeds the historical baseline by
at least $`\delta>0`$: $`\phi_{(x,\tau),k}\geq\Phi_{x}^{(k)}+\delta.`$
We denote the set of all such trajectories by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)=\left\{\tau:\phi_{(x,\tau),k}\geq\Phi_{x}^{(k)}+\delta\right\}.
``` |  |

###### Proposition 2 (Intrinsic reward favors valuable exploration).

Let
$`p_{k,\delta}(\pi;x)=\Pr_{\tau\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)\times P}\left[\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\right].`$
Then, for any policy $`\pi\in\Pi`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{\mathrm{aug}}(\pi;x)\geq J_{\mathrm{ext}}(\pi;x)+\delta\,p_{k,\delta}(\pi;x).
``` |  | (7) |

Consequently, among policies with equal extrinsic value, the augmented
objective favors policies that sample $`\delta`$-valuable trajectories
with higher probability.

###### Proof.

If $`\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)`$, then
$`R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)=\left[\phi_{(x,\tau),k}-\Phi_{x}^{(k)}\right]_{+}\geq\delta.`$
For all other trajectories, $`R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\geq 0`$.
Therefore,
$`\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)\times P}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\right]\geq\delta\,\Pr_{\tau\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)\times P}\left[\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\right].`$
Adding $`J_{\mathrm{ext}}(\pi;x)`$ to both sides proves the claim. ∎

Proposition [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 2 (Intrinsic reward favors valuable exploration). ‣ Theoretical Justification. ‣ 3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
shows that intrinsic reward breaks the degeneracy among failed
trajectories in sparse-reward settings: failures with stronger reflected
progress obtain larger augmented returns and therefore provide a
directed learning signal.

###### Proposition 3 (Monotone exploration pressure).

For every task $`x`$, the baseline sequence
$`\{\Phi_{x}^{(k)}\}_{k\geq 0}`$ is monotonically non-decreasing.
Moreover,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\phi_{(x,\tau),k}>\Phi_{x}^{(k)}.
``` |  |

Thus, as the agent’s capability improves, intrinsic reward is assigned
only to trajectories that exceed the updated threshold.

###### Proof.

By the update rule in
Equation ([5](#S3.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ 3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
$`\Phi_{x}^{(k+1)}=\max\left\{\Phi_{x}^{(k)},\bar{I}^{\mathrm{ext}}_{k}(x)\right\}\geq\Phi_{x}^{(k)}.`$
Hence, $`\{\Phi_{x}^{(k)}\}_{k\geq 0}`$ is monotonically non-decreasing.
The equivalence follows immediately from
$`R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)=\left[\phi_{(x,\tau),k}-\Phi_{x}^{(k)}\right]_{+}.`$
∎

### 3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory

The numerical exploration reward indicates *which* trajectories are
promising, but it does not explain *how* the agent should improve. To
provide reusable guidance, RetroAgent maintains a retrieval-augmented
reflection memory. Each memory entry is
$`m_{i}=(x_{i},l_{i},\tau_{i},u_{i},n_{i},d_{i})`$, where $`x_{i}`$ is
the task instruction, $`l_{i}`$ is the retrospective lesson,
$`\tau_{i}`$ is the source trajectory, $`u_{i}\in[0,1]`$ is the
empirical utility of the lesson, $`n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}`$ is its retrieval
count, and $`d_{i}\in\{\mathrm{success},\mathrm{failure}\}`$ is the
originating extrinsic outcome.

For efficient semantic retrieval, each task instruction is embedded
using a frozen sentence encoder $`\mathcal{E}`$. We embed each memory
instruction as $`\mathbf{v}_{i}=\mathcal{E}(x_{i}).`$

#### SimUtil-UCB retrieval.

Given a current task $`x`$, SimUtil-UCB scores each memory entry using
semantic relevance, empirical utility, and an exploration bonus.
Semantic relevance is measured by cosine similarity,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
s_{\mathrm{rel}}(x,x_{i})=\frac{\mathcal{E}(x)^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{i}}{\|\mathcal{E}(x)\|_{2}\|\mathbf{v}_{i}\|_{2}},
``` |  | (8) |

and entries with $`s_{\mathrm{rel}}(x,x_{i})<0.4`$ are discarded. Each
utility score is initialized to $`u_{i}=0.5`$. Whenever $`m_{i}`$ is
retrieved and the resulting episode obtains task success score
$`\hat{u}_{t}\in[0,1]`$, the utility is updated by
$`u_{i}\leftarrow(1-\beta_{\mathrm{util}})u_{i}+\beta_{\mathrm{util}}\hat{u}_{t}`$,
where $`\beta_{\mathrm{util}}\in(0,1)`$.

To avoid repeatedly exploiting only a small subset of memories,
SimUtil-UCB adds a UCB exploration bonus. Let
$`N_{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{j}n_{j}`$ be the total number of memory
retrievals. The utility-UCB value is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
u_{\mathrm{UCB}}^{(i)}=u_{i}+\kappa\sqrt{\frac{\ln N_{\mathcal{M}}}{\max(n_{i},1)}},
``` |  | (9) |

where $`\kappa>0`$ controls memory exploration and is set to $`1.0`$ in
our experiments. The final retrieval score is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S(m_{i}\mid x,\mathcal{M})=\alpha s_{\mathrm{rel}}(x,x_{i})+(1-\alpha)u_{\mathrm{UCB}}^{(i)},
``` |  | (10) |

where $`\alpha\in[0,1]`$ controls the relevance–utility trade-off. Let
$`\mathcal{K}`$ be the top-$`k`$ entries under $`S`$. The selected
lessons are concatenated as
$`l_{\mathrm{retrieved}}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{K}}l_{i}`$, yielding
the memory-augmented input
$`f_{\mathrm{memory}}(x,\mathcal{M})=x\oplus l_{\mathrm{retrieved}}`$.
After retrieval, each selected entry is marked as accessed by setting
$`n_{i}\leftarrow n_{i}+1`$ for all $`i\in\mathcal{K}`$.

#### Theoretical Justification.

We formalize reflection memory as intrinsic language feedback using the
Learning from Language Feedback (LLF) framework ([Xu et al.,
2025](#bib.bib27)). Scalar rewards provide outcome-level supervision,
whereas retrieved lessons impose trajectory-level constraints that can
distinguish task-solving hypotheses more efficiently than rewards alone.

Let $`\Pi`$ denote the policy class and $`\mathcal{H}`$ a class of
text-expressible hypotheses over task-solving strategies. Each
hypothesis $`\eta\in\mathcal{H}`$ induces a value function:
$`r_{\eta}:\Pi\to[0,1].`$ For a task $`x`$, let $`\mathcal{M}(x)`$ be
the distribution over retrieved lessons. A lesson
$`l\sim\mathcal{M}(x)`$ induces a verifier loss:

|     |                           |     |
|-----|---------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \ell(\pi,l,\eta)\in[0,1],
       ```                        |     |

where smaller values indicate that hypothesis $`\eta`$ better explains
the behavior of policy $`\pi`$ under lesson $`l`$. The memory-induced
loss and its corresponding excess loss are defined as:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
L_{\mathcal{M}(x)}(\pi,\eta)=\mathbb{E}_{l\sim\mathcal{M}(x)}\bigl[\ell(\pi,l,\eta)\bigr],
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Delta_{\mathcal{M}(x)}(\pi,\eta)=L_{\mathcal{M}(x)}(\pi,\eta)-\inf_{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}L_{\mathcal{M}(x)}(\pi,\eta^{\prime}).
``` |  |

###### Definition 4 (Memory-informative feedback).

For a task $`x`$, the retrieved lesson distribution $`\mathcal{M}(x)`$
is $`(C,\epsilon)`$-memory-informative if there exist constants $`C>0`$
and $`\epsilon\geq 0`$ such that, for all
$`\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}`$ and $`\pi\in\Pi`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\bigl|r_{\eta}(\pi)-r_{\eta^{\prime}}(\pi)\bigr|^{2}\leq C\Delta_{\mathcal{M}(x)}(\pi,\eta^{\prime})+\epsilon.
``` |  |

Definition [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Definition 4 (Memory-informative feedback). ‣ Theoretical Justification. ‣ 3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
establishes that if two hypotheses yield different policy values, the
retrieved lessons induce a detectable verifier-loss gap, bounded by an
approximation error $`\epsilon`$. Thus, reflection memory is effective
when reward-relevant disagreements are captured by language-feedback
consistency.

###### Definition 5 (Memory-conditioned transfer eluder dimension).

Given a memory distribution $`\mathcal{M}`$, verifier loss $`\ell`$, and
precision $`\epsilon>0`$, the memory-conditioned transfer eluder
dimension:
$`\dim_{\mathrm{TE}}\left(\mathcal{H},\ell,\epsilon\mid\mathcal{M}\right)`$
is the maximum length $`d`$ of a sequence
$`\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d}\in\Pi`$ such that, for some
$`\epsilon^{\prime}\geq\epsilon`$, each $`\pi_{j}`$ is
$`\epsilon^{\prime}`$-transfer independent of
$`\{\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{j-1}\}`$ under verifier losses induced by
lessons retrieved from $`\mathcal{M}`$.

This dimension quantifies the exploration burden—specifically, how many
policies must be evaluated before memory-induced language feedback
resolves relevant uncertainty.

###### Theorem 6 (Memory-guided complexity reduction).

Suppose $`\mathcal{M}(x)`$ is $`(C,\epsilon)`$-memory-informative, and
define the induced reward class
$`\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}=\{r_{\eta}:\eta\in\mathcal{H}\}`$. Following
the transfer-eluder analysis for reward-informative language
feedback ([Xu et al., 2025](#bib.bib27)), we have:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\dim_{\mathrm{TE}}\left(\mathcal{H},C\ell,\epsilon\mid\mathcal{M}\right)\leq\dim_{\mathrm{E}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}},\epsilon\right),
``` |  |

up to the approximation error in
Definition [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Definition 4 (Memory-informative feedback). ‣ Theoretical Justification. ‣ 3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").
This inequality can be strict when retrieved lessons identify
intermediate subgoals, failure modes, or corrective actions unobservable
from scalar rewards alone.

###### Proof sketch.

By
Definition [4](#Thmtheorem4 "Definition 4 (Memory-informative feedback). ‣ Theoretical Justification. ‣ 3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
any pair of hypotheses that differ in induced value on a policy also
exhibit a corresponding gap in memory-induced verifier loss, scaled by
$`C`$ and up to error $`\epsilon`$. Thus, memory feedback can
distinguish all reward-relevant hypothesis disagreements that scalar
rewards can. Consequently, any sequence remaining transfer-independent
under the scaled verifier $`C\ell`$ cannot exceed the length of the
longest sequence remaining independent under the induced reward class
$`\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{H}}`$. Strict improvement occurs because a
single explanatory lesson can eliminate multiple hypotheses that sparse
scalar rewards cannot distinguish. ∎

Theorem [6](#Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 6 (Memory-guided complexity reduction). ‣ Theoretical Justification. ‣ 3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
formalizes reflection memory’s role in reducing exploration complexity:
retrieved lessons do not merely provide context; they actively constrain
the set of plausible task-solving hypotheses.

###### Proposition 7 (SimUtil-UCB retrieval behavior).

Assume $`u_{i}`$ is an unbiased or asymptotically consistent estimate of
memory $`m_{i}`$’s expected downstream utility on semantically related
tasks, and that $`s_{\mathrm{rel}}(x,x_{i})`$ is bounded. SimUtil-UCB
exhibits the following properties: $`(\textup{\it i})`$ Among memories
with comparable semantic relevance, entries with higher empirical
utility are preferred as $`n_{i}`$ grows. $`(\textup{\it ii})`$ Among
memories with comparable empirical utility, entries with higher semantic
relevance are preferred. $`(\textup{\it iii})`$ Memories with lower
retrieval counts receive larger exploration bonuses.

###### Proof.

The retrieval score is defined as:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S(m_{i}\mid x,\mathcal{M})=\alpha s_{\mathrm{rel}}(x,x_{i})+(1-\alpha)\left(u_{i}+\kappa\sqrt{\frac{\ln N_{\mathcal{M}}}{\max(n_{i},1)}}\right),
``` |  |

where $`N_{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{j}n_{j}`$. For a fixed semantic relevance,
increasing $`u_{i}`$ increases $`S`$, favoring higher-utility memories.
For a fixed utility and retrieval count, increasing
$`s_{\mathrm{rel}}(x,x_{i})`$ increases $`S`$, favoring more relevant
memories. Finally, the exploration term decreases monotonically with
$`n_{i}`$, ensuring under-tested memories receive a larger bonus. As
$`n_{i}\to\infty`$, this bonus vanishes, and retrieval is governed
strictly by semantic relevance and empirical utility, adhering to the
optimism-under-uncertainty principle ([Auer et al., 2002](#bib.bib47)).
∎

### 3.4 Policy Optimization with Dual Intrinsic Feedback

RetroAgent is compatible with a broad class of RL algorithms. In this
work, we instantiate it with GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024b](#bib.bib25)),
adapted to incorporate dual intrinsic feedback into multi-turn
trajectory optimization. We describe the trajectory generation
procedure, the decision-making objective, and the optional
self-reflection objective in turn.

#### Trajectory Generation with Memory Augmentation.

For each task instruction $`x`$ from $`\mathcal{D}`$, we generate $`N`$
trajectories
under $`\Pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(\cdot\mid x,\,\mathcal{M})\times P`$
(Equation [2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ Problem Formulation. ‣ 3.1 General Overview ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
The first $`N/2`$ are sampled from the base
policy, $`\tau^{(i)}\sim\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(\cdot\mid x)\times P`$,
and the remaining $`N/2`$ from the memory-augmented
policy, $`\tau^{(i)}\sim\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}\!\bigl(\cdot\mid f_{\text{memory}}(x,\mathcal{M})\bigr)\times P`$.
Each
trajectory $`\tau^{(i)}=(s_{0}^{(i)},a_{0}^{(i)},\dots,s_{T_{i}-1}^{(i)},a_{T_{i}-1}^{(i)})`$
is a state–action sequence of length $`T_{i}`$. This partition lets the
agent leverage past experience via memory retrieval while retaining the
capacity for independent exploration, facilitating continuous policy
adaptation.

1: Training dataset $`\mathcal{D}`$, Sentence encoder $`\mathcal{E}`$,
hyperparameters
$`\alpha,\beta_{\text{util}},\kappa,\lambda_{\text{reflect}}`$.

2: Initialize memory buffer $`\mathcal{M}\leftarrow\emptyset`$, policy
parameters $`\theta`$, historical baselines $`\Phi_{x}\leftarrow 0`$ for
all $`x\in\mathcal{D}`$.

3: for each training iteration do

4:   Sample task instruction $`x\sim\mathcal{D}`$.

5:   % 1. Memory Retrieval (SimUtil-UCB)

6:   if $`\mathcal{M}\neq\emptyset`$ then

7:    Compute semantic relevance $`s_{\mathrm{rel}}`$ via cosine
similarity using $`\mathcal{E}(x)`$.

8:    Retrieve top-$`k`$ entries $`\mathcal{K}`$ maximizing
$`S(m_{i}\mid x,\mathcal{M})=\alpha s_{\mathrm{rel}}+(1-\alpha)u_{\mathrm{util-UCB}}^{(i)}`$.

9:    Form augmented input
$`f_{\text{memory}}(x,\mathcal{M})=x\oplus l_{\text{retrieved}}`$.

10:    Increment access counts: $`n_{i}\leftarrow n_{i}+1`$ for all
$`i\in\mathcal{K}`$.

11:   end if

12:   % 2. Trajectory Generation

13:   Generate $`N/2`$ trajectories $`\tau`$ via base policy
$`\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(\cdot\mid x)`$.

14:   Generate $`N/2`$ trajectories $`\tau`$ via memory-augmented policy
$`\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}\!\bigl(\cdot\mid f_{\text{memory}}(x,\mathcal{M})\bigr)`$.

15:   % 3. Self-Reflection & Intrinsic Feedback

16:   for each trajectory $`\tau^{(i)}`$ in the $`N`$ rollouts do

17:    Observe extrinsic reward $`R^{\text{ext},(i)}`$ and outcome
$`I^{\text{ext},(i)}`$.

18:    Generate reflection tuple
$`z^{(i)}=(\phi_{(x,\tau)}^{(i)},c^{(i)},l^{(i)})`$ via reflection
function $`f_{\text{reflect}}`$ or policy $`\varphi_{\theta}`$.

19:    Compute capability-evolution intrinsic reward:
$`R^{\text{int},(i)}\leftarrow\max\!\bigl(0,\;\phi_{(x,\tau)}^{(i)}-\Phi_{x}\bigr)`$.

20:    Store new memory entry $`m^{(i)}`$ into buffer $`\mathcal{M}`$.

21:   end for

22:   Update task baseline:
$`\Phi_{x}\leftarrow\max\!\bigl(\Phi_{x},\;\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}I^{\text{ext},(j)}\bigr)`$.

23:   Update utilities $`u_{i}`$ for retrieved entries
$`i\in\mathcal{K}`$ via EMA:
$`u_{i}\leftarrow(1-\beta_{\text{util}})u_{i}+\beta_{\text{util}}\hat{u}_{t}`$.

24:   % 4. Policy Optimization (Dual Feedback)

25:   Compute advantages $`\hat{A}^{(i)}`$ using composite returns
$`G^{(i)}=\sum\gamma^{t}\bigl(R^{\text{ext},(i)}+R^{\text{int},(i)}\bigr)`$.

26:   Update decision-making policy $`\theta`$ by maximizing
$`\mathcal{J}_{\text{Decision-Making}}(\theta)`$ via GRPO.

27:   if using RL-Trained Reflection Variant then

28:    Compute reflection reward:
$`R^{\text{reflect},(i)}\leftarrow R^{\text{ext},(i)}\cdot\mathbf{1}\{c=I^{\mathrm{ext}}\}`$.

29:    Update reflection policy $`\varphi_{\theta}`$ by maximizing
$`\mathcal{J}_{\text{Self-Reflection}}(\theta)`$ via REINFORCE.

30:   end if

31: end for

Algorithm 1 RetroAgent Training Framework

#### Decision-Making Objective.

Since both $`R^{\text{ext}}`$ and $`R^{\text{int}}`$ are uniform across
time steps
(Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 General Overview ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
the discounted return reduces to a trajectory-level
scalar $`G^{(i)}=\sum_{t=0}^{T_{i}-1}\gamma^{t}\bigl(R^{\text{ext},(i)}+R^{\text{int},(i)}\bigr)`$,
and every step within a trajectory shares the same group-relative
advantage:
$`\hat{A}^{(i)}=\frac{G^{(i)}-\operatorname{mean}\!\bigl(\{G^{(1)},\dots,G^{(N)}\}\bigr)}{\operatorname{std}\!\bigl(\{G^{(1)},\dots,G^{(N)}\}\bigr)}.`$

Defining the per-token importance ratio as
$`\rho_{t,j}^{(i)}(\theta)=\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a_{t,j}^{(i)}\mid s_{t}^{(i)},\,a_{t,<j}^{(i)})}{\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(a_{t,j}^{(i)}\mid s_{t}^{(i)},\,a_{t,<j}^{(i)})}`$,
the decision-making objective is formulated as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{Decision-Making}}(\theta)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D},\,\{\tau^{(i)}\}\sim\Pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(\cdot\mid x,\,\mathcal{M})\times P}\Bigg[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{T_{i}}\sum_{t=0}^{T_{i}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{|a_{t}^{(i)}|}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{t,j}^{\text{clip}}\!\bigl(\theta,\,\hat{A}^{(i)}\bigr)`$ |  | (11) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle-\beta\,D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\bigl[\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s_{t}^{(i)})\,\|\,\pi_{\text{ref}}(\cdot\mid s_{t}^{(i)})\bigr]\bigg)\Bigg],`$ |  |  |

where $`|a_{t}^{(i)}|`$ denotes the number of tokens in action
$`a_{t}^{(i)}`$. Following [Liu et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib1), we remove
the length normalization term $`\frac{1}{|a_{t}^{(i)}|}`$. The clipped
surrogate function is defined as
$`\mathcal{L}^{\text{clip}}_{t,j}\!\left(\theta,\,\hat{A}^{(i)}\right)=\min\left(\rho_{t,j}^{(i)}(\theta)\,\hat{A}^{(i)},\;\operatorname{clip}\!\left(\rho_{t,j}^{(i)}(\theta),\,1{-}\epsilon_{\text{clip}},\,1{+}\epsilon_{\text{clip}}\right)\hat{A}^{(i)}\right)`$,
where $`\epsilon_{\text{clip}}`$ bounds the policy update and $`\beta`$
controls the KL divergence regularization toward the reference policy
$`\pi_{\text{ref}}`$. For the *in-context* self-reflection variant, the
total objective is simply
$`\mathcal{J}_{\text{RetroAgent}}(\theta)=\mathcal{J}_{\text{Decision-Making}}(\theta)`$.

#### Self-Reflection Objective (for RL-Trained Variant).

The *RL-trained* variant additionally optimizes the reflection
policy $`\varphi_{\theta}`$. For each trajectory $`\tau^{(i)}`$,
$`\varphi_{\theta}`$ generates a reflection sequence
$`z^{(i)}=(\phi^{(i)}_{(x,\tau)},\,c^{(i)},\,l^{(i)})`$. The success
prediction component $`c^{(i)}`$ is scored by $`R^{\text{reflect},(i)}`$
(Equation ([3](#S3.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ Hindsight Self-Reflection. ‣ 3.1 General Overview ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"))).
We optimize $`\varphi_{\theta}`$ using REINFORCE ([Williams,
1992](#bib.bib24)):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{J}_{\text{Self-Reflection}}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\{z^{(i)}\sim\varphi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(\cdot\mid\tau^{(i)})\}}\!\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{|z^{(i)}|}\log\varphi_{\theta}\!\bigl(z_{j}^{(i)}\mid\tau^{(i)},\,z_{<j}^{(i)}\bigr)\cdot R^{\text{reflect},(i)}\biggr],
``` |  | (12) |

where $`|z^{(i)}|`$ is the token length of the reflection sequence.
Gradients from this objective do not propagate through trajectory
generation. The full RL-trained objective is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{J}_{\text{RetroAgent}}(\theta)=\mathcal{J}_{\text{Decision-Making}}(\theta)+\lambda_{\text{reflect}}\cdot\mathcal{J}_{\text{Self-Reflection}}(\theta).
``` |  | (13) |

We summarize the RetroAgent framework in
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Trajectory Generation with Memory Augmentation. ‣ 3.4 Policy Optimization with Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
and provide theoretical justifications in
Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

## 4 Experiments

### 4.1 Experimental Setup

#### Environments.

We evaluate RetroAgent across four distinct agentic tasks:
$`(\textup{\it i})`$ ALFWorld ([Shridhar et al., 2021](#bib.bib36)), a
text-based embodied environment where agents complete household tasks
through navigation and object interaction. We assess both
in-distribution (seen rooms) and out-of-distribution (unseen rooms)
generalization. $`(\textup{\it ii})`$ Webshop ([Yao et al.,
2022b](#bib.bib35)), a simulated e-commerce environment requiring agents
to navigate a web interface to purchase products matching user
specifications. $`(\textup{\it iii})`$ Sokoban ([Racanière et al.,
2017](#bib.bib33)), a planning-heavy puzzle task where agents must push
boxes to target locations. Due to the irreversible nature of pushing
actions, errors often render puzzles unsolvable. Complexity is governed
by board size and box count; we train on $`6\times 6`$ boards with 2
boxes, following [Jiang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib3).
$`(\textup{\it iv})`$ MineSweeper ([Li et al., 2024](#bib.bib34)), a
logic-based puzzle requiring agents to identify mine locations using
numerical clues. Difficulty is controlled by board size and mine
density; we train on $`6\times 6`$ boards with 3 mines. We report
Success Rate across all tasks, supplemented by Task Score for WebShop.

#### Compared Methods.

We evaluate RetroAgentgent against four categories of competitive
baselines, reporting results averaged over three independent runs:
$`(\textup{\it i})`$ Prompting-based methods: We compare against
ReAct ([Yao et al., 2022c](#bib.bib4)) and Reflexion ([Shinn et al.,
2023](#bib.bib6)), the latter of which incorporates an in-context
self-reflection mechanism for iterative refinement.
$`(\textup{\it ii})`$ RL algorithms: We include REINFORCE Leave-One-Out
(RLOO) ([Kool et al., 2019](#bib.bib40); [Ahmadian et al.,
2024](#bib.bib71)), GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024b](#bib.bib25)), and
Group-in-Group Policy Optimization (GiGPO) ([Feng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib5)). GiGPO represents the current state-of-the-art by
utilizing anchor-state grouping for fine-grained credit assignment.
$`(\textup{\it iii})`$ RL-based frameworks: This category includes
memory-augmented methods such as MemRL ([Zhang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib23)) (which updates a memory bank while keeping the policy
frozen), EvolveR ([Wu et al., 2025](#bib.bib21)) (which integrates raw
trajectories into optimization), and Mem0 ([Chhikara et al.,
2025](#bib.bib20))+GRPO and SimpleMem ([Liu et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib17))+GRPO, (which incorporate persistent memory into the
training process). We also compare against SkillRL ([Xia et al.,
2026](#bib.bib22)), a hybrid approach (supervised finetuning and RL)
that induces actionable skills via a teacher model to guide the
student’s policy optimization, and GRPO with EMPG ([Wang et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib77)), which utilizes entropy-modulated policy gradients
for long-horizon optimization. $`(\textup{\it iv})`$ A Meta-RL
framework ([Beck et al., 2025](#bib.bib72)): We compare against
LaMer ([Jiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)), which leverages a multi-episode
structure to foster active exploration and robust adaptation within a
meta-learning context.

#### Implementation Details.

We evaluate RetroAgent on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct ([Qwen et al.,
2025](#bib.bib70)) and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ([Grattafiori et al.,
2024](#bib.bib69)). Although RetroAgent is generally compatible with
various RL algorithms, we adopt GRPO as the default and implement our
framework by adapting the open-source Verl training library ([Sheng et
al., 2024](#bib.bib68)). We employ the task prompts from [Feng et al.
(2025)](#bib.bib5) to enable decision-making via the ReAct format ([Yao
et al., 2022c](#bib.bib4)), in which the model generates step-by-step
reasoning before its corresponding action. At training time, the agent
distills lessons as memories from trajectories on the training set; at
test time, the agent leverages these memories for task completion on the
test set. Detailed hyperparameter settings and training configurations
are provided in
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

Table 1: Main results across four benchmarks, averaged over three
independent runs (mean $`\pm`$ standard deviation). All improvements are
statistically significant with $`p<0.01`$. Results marked with ^(∗) are
cited from prior work ([Xia et al., 2026](#bib.bib22); [Feng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib5); [Wang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib77)). Unless otherwise
specified, all training frameworks use the GRPO algorithm. “Success” and
“Score” denote Success Rate and Task Score, respectively. w/ Teacher
Model indicates methods that require a teacher model for skill
induction.

We present the main results in
Table [1](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 4.2 Main Results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
and summarize the key findings below.

Retrospective dual intrinsic feedback improves policy evolution.
RetroAgent achieves state-of-the-art performance on all four benchmarks,
outperforming GRPO by $`+14.4`$, $`+12.0`$, $`+21.4`$, and $`+8.6`$
percentage points on ALFWorld, WebShop, Sokoban, and MineSweeper,
respectively. These gains show that augmenting extrinsic rewards with
hindsight-generated intrinsic feedback enables more effective learning
than success-driven RL alone. On WebShop, RetroAgent further surpasses
the strongest baselines, GiGPO and SkillRL, by $`+6.1`$–$`6.2\%`$,
confirming the benefit of combining progress-aware exploration with
explicit experience reuse.

Numerical and language feedback are complementary. RetroAgent
consistently outperforms memory-augmented methods, including MemRL,
EvolveR, SimpleMem+GRPO, and SkillRL, showing that textual memory alone
is insufficient without progress-aware exploration signals. It also
exceeds GRPO w/ EMPG, which uses uncertainty-based intrinsic rewards,
indicating that numerical signals alone miss reusable trajectory-level
knowledge. These results validate the complementary roles of intrinsic
numerical feedback for exploration and intrinsic language feedback for
experience reuse.

Distilled lessons are more useful than raw trajectories. RetroAgent
substantially outperforms EvolveR, which reuses raw trajectories as
in-context demonstrations; on WebShop, RetroAgent achieves
$`78.9`$–$`82.3\%`$ success versus $`17.6\%`$ for EvolveR. This suggests
that raw trajectories may contain noisy or task-specific details,
whereas retrospective self-reflection produces concise and transferable
lessons for future decisions.

RL-trained self-reflection further improves performance. Jointly
training the self-reflection policy further improves RetroAgent,
reaching $`95.6\%`$, $`82.3\%`$, and $`38.3\%`$ success on ALFWorld,
WebShop, and Sokoban, respectively. This indicates that reflective
feedback generation can itself be optimized to better support policy
evolution.

### 4.3 Test-Time Adaptation and Generalisation

(a) Test-time adaptation on WebShop (ID).

(b) Test-time adaptation on ALFWorld (OOD).

Figure 3: Test-time adaptation in an in-distribution (ID) setting on
WebShop and an out-of-distribution (OOD) setting on ALFWorld.

#### Test-Time Adaptation.

Following [Jiang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib3), we evaluate test-time
adaptation with $`\mathrm{Discovery}@k`$ ([Hübotter et al.,
2026](#bib.bib18)), defined as the probability of solving a task within
$`k`$ attempts:
$`\mathrm{Discovery}@k:=P\!\left(\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}r(y_{i}\mid x)=1\right).`$
Results are shown in
Figure [3](#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 4.3 Test-Time Adaptation and Generalisation ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

RetroAgent enables rapid test-time adaptation. RetroAgent reaches
near-perfect discovery within three attempts in both in-distribution
WebShop ($`82.3\%\to 99.0\%`$) and out-of-distribution ALFWorld
($`92.9\%\to 100.0\%`$) settings, consistently outperforming the Meta-RL
baseline LaMer. The margin over LaMer further increases with $`k`$ in
OOD settings, suggesting that retrospective reasoning better exploits
repeated attempts.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Method |     Memory  Retrieval | WebShop |  |  |
|  |  | Discovery@1 (%) | Discovery@2 (%) | Discovery@3 (%) |
| GRPO (Baseline) | – | $`66.9_{\pm 1.2}`$ | $`87.8_{\pm 1.8}`$ | $`97.1_{\pm 0.5}`$ |
| RetroAgent (In-Context) | $`\times`$ | $`76.8_{\pm 1.6}`$ | $`91.9_{\pm 1.2}`$ | $`98.4_{\pm 0.0}`$ |
| RetroAgent (RL-Trained) | $`\times`$ | $`77.1_{\pm 1.6}`$ | $`91.7_{\pm 1.2}`$ | $`99.0_{\pm 0.5}`$ |
| RetroAgent (In-Context) | $`\checkmark`$ | $`78.9_{\pm 3.6}`$ | $`93.0_{\pm 1.4}`$ | $`97.9_{\pm 0.5}`$ |
| RetroAgent (RL-Trained) | $`\checkmark`$ | $`\mathbf{82.3_{\pm 1.6}}`$ | $`\mathbf{93.0_{\pm 0.8}}`$ | $`\mathbf{99.0_{\pm 0.5}}`$ |

Table 2: Impact of memory retrieval on test-time adaptation.

RetroAgent internalizes intrinsic feedback during training.
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Test-Time Adaptation. ‣ 4.3 Test-Time Adaptation and Generalisation ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
ablates test-time memory retrieval. Removing retrieval causes only minor
drops in $`\mathrm{Discovery}@1`$ and $`\mathrm{Discovery}@2`$ (*e.g.,*
$`78.9\%\to 76.8\%`$ for in-context self-reflection), while
$`\mathrm{Discovery}@3`$ remains unchanged. This suggests that dual
intrinsic feedback is largely absorbed into the policy parameters,
rather than relying on memory access at inference time.

caption](2603.08561v6/minesweeper_n_mine4_pass_k_broken_axis_plot.png)

instances (trained with 3 mines, evaluated with 4 mines).

![Refer to
caption](2603.08561v6/minesweeper_n_mines_comparison_highest_only.png)

(b) Generalization across increasing difficulty levels (evaluated with

Figure 4: Robustness to challenging tasks on MineSweeper.

#### Robustness to Challenging Tasks.

Following [Jiang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib3), we assess robustness on
MineSweeper under two harder-than-training settings
(Figure [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Test-Time Adaptation. ‣ 4.3 Test-Time Adaptation and Generalisation ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")):
$`(\textup{\it i})`$ increasing the mine count from $`3`$ to $`4`$, and
$`(\textup{\it ii})`$ varying the mine count from $`3`$ to $`5`$.

RetroAgent remains robust under increased difficulty. RetroAgent
consistently outperforms all baselines in both settings, showing rapid
adaptation to harder instances
(Figure [4(a)](#S4.F4.sf1 "Figure 4(a) ‣ Figure 4 ‣ Test-Time Adaptation. ‣ 4.3 Test-Time Adaptation and Generalisation ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"))
and graceful degradation as task difficulty increases
(Figure [4(b)](#S4.F4.sf2 "Figure 4(b) ‣ Figure 4 ‣ Test-Time Adaptation. ‣ 4.3 Test-Time Adaptation and Generalisation ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).

(a) Completion scores via single induction.

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/pairwise_reward_calibration.png)

(b) Completion scores via pairwise induction.

single-trajectory (single) vs. pairwise-trajectory (pairwise) induction
for Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on WebShop.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Method | Hallucination Rate (%) |  | Estimated Utility Score (%) |  |  |  |  |  |
|  |     Failure  ($`\downarrow`$) |     Success  ($`\downarrow`$) | Failure |  |  | Success |  |  |
|  |  |  | Low ($`\downarrow`$) | Med ($`-`$) | High ($`\uparrow`$) | Low ($`\downarrow`$) | Med ($`-`$) | High ($`\uparrow`$) |
| Single Induction | 8.8 | 15.1 | 8.8 | 78.2 | 12.9 | 12.2 | 75.6 | 12.2 |
| Pairwise Induction | 3.8 | 11.9 | 3.1 | 76.7 | 20.1 | 6.2 | 76.2 | 17.6 |

Table 3: Quality of lessons (*i.e.,* memories) generated via
single-trajectory vs. pairwise-trajectory induction, as assessed by
GPT-4o.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Method |     Augmentation  Ratio | WebShop |  |
|  |  | Task Score (%) | Success Rate (%) |
| GRPO | — | 75.5_(±3.6) | 66.9_(±1.2) |
| + Single Induction | 100% (Full Group) | 81.3_(±2.6) | 70.3_(±2.1) |
| + Pairwise Induction | 100% (Full Group) | 82.3_(±1.3) | 72.9_(±1.6) |
| + Pairwise Induction | 050% (Half Group) | $`\mathbf{82.4}_{\pm 2.9}`$ | $`\mathbf{75.3}_{\pm 4.3}`$ |

Table 4: Effect of induction method and augmentation ratio on GRPO
performance. Augmentation Ratio denotes the fraction of sampled
trajectories per prompt that receive memory-augmented generation; the
remaining trajectories are sampled without augmentation.

The effectiveness of RetroAgent depends on the quality of
self-reflection, which determines both the accuracy of intrinsic
numerical feedback and the utility of intrinsic language feedback. We
compare single-trajectory and pairwise-trajectory induction under the
in-context self-reflection mechanism.

For numerical feedback, we use GPT-4o subtask completion scores ([OpenAI
et al., 2024](#bib.bib67)) as oracle estimates and measure correlation
with each induction method. For language feedback, we use GPT-4o to
evaluate lesson quality. We further assess downstream impact by
augmenting GRPO with lessons from each method, retrieved by semantic
relevance to the task prompt
(Table [4](#S4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
Details are provided in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Superiority of Pairwise Induction over Single Induction ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

Pairwise induction yields more accurate and useful self-reflection.
Pairwise-trajectory induction produces intrinsic numerical feedback that
better matches oracle subtask completion scores, as shown by its closer
alignment with the dashed oracle curve in
Figure [5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").
It also generates higher-quality language feedback, with lower
hallucination rates and higher estimated utility
(Table [3](#S4.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
These improvements translate into better downstream optimization: GRPO
augmented with pairwise-induction lessons outperforms the
single-induction variant ($`72.9\%`$ vs. $`70.3\%`$;
Table [4](#S4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).

Preserving unguided exploration prevents premature convergence.
Table [4](#S4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
shows that half-group memory augmentation outperforms full-group
augmentation ($`75.3\%`$ vs. $`72.9\%`$ success). This indicates that
applying memory-guided generation to all sampled trajectories reduces
diversity and can promote premature convergence to suboptimal
strategies.

| Method |     Discounted  Returns |     Reward  Type | WebShop |  |
|  |  |  | Task Score (%) | Success Rate (%) |
| GRPO (Baseline) | – | Extrinsic | $`75.5_{\pm 3.6}`$ | $`66.9_{\pm 1.2}`$ |
| GRPO | ✓ | Extrinsic | $`84.2_{\pm 0.2}`$ | $`74.7_{\pm 2.7}`$ |
| + Progress-Guided Rewards | ✓ | Extrinsic | $`84.2_{\pm 1.7}`$ | $`75.0_{\pm 3.1}`$ |
| + Capability-Evolution Rewards | ✓ | Extrinsic & Intrinsic | $`\mathbf{88.2}_{\pm 2.1}`$ | $`\mathbf{79.7}_{\pm 3.1}`$ |

Table 5: Impact of discounted returns and intrinsic reward shaping on
GRPO. Capability-evolution rewards denote the intrinsic numerical
feedback described in
Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

![Refer to
caption](2603.08561v6/impact_of_intrinsic_rewards_credit_assignment.png)

(a) Impact of capability-evolution rewards.

![Refer to
caption](2603.08561v6/impact_of_retrieved_memory_comparison.png)

(b) Impact of memory-retrieval strategies.

Figure 6: Valid-set performance dynamics on WebShop when augmenting GRPO
with intrinsic numerical feedback (a) or intrinsic language feedback
(b).

We study the effects of discounted returns and intrinsic reward shaping
on GRPO.
Table [5](#S4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ 4.5 Effect of Intrinsic Numerical Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
reports evaluation results, and
Figure [6(a)](#S4.F6.sf1 "Figure 6(a) ‣ Figure 6 ‣ 4.5 Effect of Intrinsic Numerical Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
shows validation dynamics. As an additional baseline, we consider
*progress-guided rewards*, which replace the potential score
$`\phi_{(x,\tau)}`$ in
Equation [6](#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3.2 Intrinsic Numerical Feedback for Directed Exploration ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
with the binary environment success score $`I^{\mathrm{Ext}}`$, thereby
grounding the rectified gain in extrinsic outcomes rather than
self-assessed progress.

Intrinsic numerical feedback improves policy optimization. As shown in
Table [5](#S4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ 4.5 Effect of Intrinsic Numerical Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
using discounted returns to compute trajectory-level advantages improves
GRPO by $`+8.7`$ percentage points in task score and $`+7.8`$ in success
rate on WebShop. Adding capability-evolution rewards further increases
performance to $`88.2\%`$ task score and $`79.7\%`$ success rate, with
consistent gains from step 25 onward
(Figure [6(a)](#S4.F6.sf1 "Figure 6(a) ‣ Figure 6 ‣ 4.5 Effect of Intrinsic Numerical Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
Capability-evolution rewards also outperform progress-guided rewards,
indicating that self-reflection-based potential scores provide richer
shaping signals than binary extrinsic outcomes alone.

### 4.6 Effect of Intrinsic Language Feedback

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Method |     Discounted  Returns |     Retrieval  Strategy | WebShop |  |
|  |  |  | Task Score (%) | Success Rate (%) |
| GRPO (Baseline) | – | – | $`75.5_{\pm 3.6}`$ | $`66.9_{\pm 1.2}`$ |
| GRPO | ✓ | – | $`84.2_{\pm 0.2}`$ | $`74.7_{\pm 2.7}`$ |
| + Memory Retrieval | ✓ | Similarity | $`79.1_{\pm 7.1}`$ | $`70.1_{\pm 5.5}`$ |
| + Memory Retrieval | ✓ | Similarity & Utility | $`78.4_{\pm 11.4}`$ | $`69.5_{\pm 8.7}`$ |
| + Memory Retrieval | ✓ | SimUtil-UCB | $`\mathbf{86.4\pm 1.8}`$ | $`\mathbf{78.6\pm 1.6}`$ |

Table 6: Impact of intrinsic language feedback on GRPO using different
memory-retrieval strategies. SimUtil-UCB denotes the our proposed memory
retrieval strategy
(Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).

Having shown in
Section [4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
that intrinsic language feedback improves policy optimization, we
evaluate SimUtil-UCB against two ablations: similarity-based retrieval
(Criterion 1 only) and similarity–utility retrieval (Criteria 1–2,
without the exploration bonus).
Table [6](#S4.T6 "Table 6 ‣ 4.6 Effect of Intrinsic Language Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
reports final performance, and
Figure [6(b)](#S4.F6.sf2 "Figure 6(b) ‣ Figure 6 ‣ 4.5 Effect of Intrinsic Numerical Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
shows validation dynamics. All experiments use half-group memory
augmentation.

Balancing relevance, utility, and exploration is critical. As shown in
Table [6](#S4.T6 "Table 6 ‣ 4.6 Effect of Intrinsic Language Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
discounted returns improve GRPO, but adding memories retrieved by
similarity-only or similarity–utility criteria degrades performance.
This contrasts with
Table [4](#S4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
where similarity-based retrieval improves standard GRPO without
discounted returns, suggesting that discounted returns may amplify
low-quality memory-guided behaviors. In contrast, SimUtil-UCB
consistently improves performance, reaching an $`86.4\%`$ task score and
a $`78.6\%`$ success rate
(Table [6](#S4.T6 "Table 6 ‣ 4.6 Effect of Intrinsic Language Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback");
Figure [6(b)](#S4.F6.sf2 "Figure 6(b) ‣ Figure 6 ‣ 4.5 Effect of Intrinsic Numerical Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
By adding the UCB exploration bonus
(Equation [9](#S3.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ SimUtil-UCB retrieval. ‣ 3.3 Intrinsic Language Feedback via Reflection Memory ‣ 3 RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
SimUtil-UCB avoids over-reliance on semantically similar or historically
high-utility memories and promotes broader use of under-explored
lessons.

Figure [7](#S4.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 4.6 Effect of Intrinsic Language Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
further analyzes retrieval frequency. Each memory instance is
initialized with count $`1`$, which increments after retrieval.
SimUtil-UCB distributes access more evenly, with most instances
retrieved about $`5`$ times
(Figure [7(c)](#S4.F7.sf3 "Figure 7(c) ‣ Figure 7 ‣ 4.6 Effect of Intrinsic Language Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
whereas similarity-based retrieval concentrates on a small subset, with
many instances exceeding $`15`$ retrievals
(Figure [7(a)](#S4.F7.sf1 "Figure 7(a) ‣ Figure 7 ‣ 4.6 Effect of Intrinsic Language Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
This confirms that the UCB bonus diversifies memory usage and
contributes to stronger final performance.

(a) Similarity-based retrieval.

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/webshop_kde_utility.png)

(b) Similarity & utility.

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/webshop_kde_utility_ucb.png)

(c) SimUtil-UCB.
````
