````math
### 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Method |     Intrinsic  Feedback |     Self-Reflection  Mechanism | WebShop |  |
|  |  |  | Task Score (%) | Success Rate (%) |
| GRPO (Baseline) | – | – | $`75.5_{\pm 3.6}`$ | $`66.9_{\pm 1.2}`$ |
| + Capability-Evolution Rewards | Numerical | Pairwise Induction | $`88.2_{\pm 2.1}`$ | $`79.7_{\pm 3.1}`$ |
| + SimUtil-UCB Memory Retrieval | Language | Pairwise Induction | $`86.4_{\pm 1.8}`$ | $`78.6_{\pm 1.6}`$ |
| RetroAgent (In-Context) | Dual | Pairwise Induction | $`87.6_{\pm 2.1}`$ | $`78.9_{\pm 3.6}`$ |
| RetroAgent (RL-Trained) | Dual | Pairwise Induction | $`87.0_{\pm 1.4}`$ | $`77.1_{\pm 1.0}`$ |
| RetroAgent (RL-Trained) | Dual | Single Induction | $`\mathbf{88.9_{\pm 1.3}}`$ | $`\mathbf{82.3_{\pm 1.6}}`$ |

Table 7: Individual and combined effects of intrinsic numerical and
language feedback under different self-reflection mechanisms on WebShop.
Rows above the dashed line ablate each feedback type in isolation; rows
below combine both (Dual).

(a) Valid-set performance over the course of training.

(b) Reflection accuracy over the course of training, smoothed with
exponential moving average (EMA) ([Klinker, 2011](#bib.bib26)).

Figure 8: In-context vs. RL-trained self-reflection mechanisms in
RetroAgent on WebShop.

We present results for combining intrinsic numerical and language
feedback in
Table [7](#S4.T7 "Table 7 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
and compare in-context versus RL-trained reflection mechanisms in
Figure [8](#S4.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

Combining dual intrinsic feedback facilitates superior agentic
reasoning. As shown in
Table [7](#S4.T7 "Table 7 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
RetroAgent achieves notable performance gains (*e.g.,* $`\approx+3\%`$
success rate) by integrating dual intrinsic feedback compared to using
either capability-evolution rewards or SimUtil-UCB memory retrieval in
isolation. The in-context variant, however, slightly underperforms GRPO
with capability-evolution rewards only, suggesting that simultaneous
exploration signals from both feedback channels might interfere with
each other during action selection.

Joint optimization preserves reflection quality and improves RL
training. In
Figure [8(b)](#S4.F8.sf2 "Figure 8(b) ‣ Figure 8 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
the reflection accuracy of the in-context variant declines steadily as
the policy improves (orange curve), even though extrinsic success
signals remain available. In contrast, the RL-trained self-reflection
mechanism maintains accuracy throughout training (blue curve). Although
accuracy dips slightly before step 75—likely because decision-making
policy improvement temporarily outpaces reflection adaptation—it
recovers and increases steadily thereafter. The initial gap relative to
the in-context baseline arises because the RL-trained variant uses
single induction, which is less informative than pairwise induction
(consistent with
Section [4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Analysis of In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).

We validate the choice of single induction by comparing it against a
pairwise variant that conditions on a reference trajectory:
$`z=f_{\text{reflect}}(\tau_{\text{ref}},\tau)`$. Although including
$`\tau_{\text{ref}}`$ yields the highest reflection accuracy (green
curve,
Figure [8(b)](#S4.F8.sf2 "Figure 8(b) ‣ Figure 8 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
it does not improve task performance
(Table [7](#S4.T7 "Table 7 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
This discrepancy suggests that contrastive comparison enables the
reflector to infer outcomes from relative differences between
trajectories rather than developing robust standalone evaluation
capability.

### 4.8 Effect of Intrinsic Feedback on Exploration

Both intrinsic numerical and language feedback are designed to improve
RL by guiding exploration: capability-evolution rewards encourage
promising action sequences, while retrieved lessons discourage repeated
failures and reinforce effective strategies. We test this hypothesis on
the WebShop test set under three settings: $`(\textup{\it i})`$ GRPO
with capability-evolution rewards only, $`(\textup{\it ii})`$ GRPO with
SimUtil-UCB memory retrieval only, and $`(\textup{\it iii})`$ RetroAgent
with in-context or RL-trained self-reflection. We quantify trajectory
diversity using the Vendi Score ([Friedman and Dieng, 2023](#bib.bib15))
over both successful and failed trajectories.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Method |     Intrinsic  Feedback | Vendi Score ($`\uparrow`$) |  |
|  |  | Successful Traj. | Failed Traj. |
| Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct | – | 0.00^(\*) | 1.89 |
| GRPO (Baseline) | – | 1.85 | 1.71 |
|    + Capability-Evolution Rewards | Numerical | 2.04 | 1.82 |
|    + SimUtil-UCB Memory Retrieval | Language | 2.13 | 1.97 |
| RetroAgent (In-Context Self-Reflection) | Dual | 2.01 | 1.78 |
| RetroAgent (RL-Trained Self-Reflection) | Dual | 2.20 | 1.94 |

Table 8: Impact of intrinsic feedback on trajectory diversity on
WebShop, measured by the Vendi Score ([Friedman and Dieng,
2023](#bib.bib15)). A score of 0.00 for Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct indicates
that fewer than two successful trajectories were generated, precluding
diversity measurement.

Intrinsic feedback promotes useful exploration. All intrinsic-feedback
variants obtain higher Vendi Scores on successful trajectories than
GRPO, indicating more diverse successful strategies. The in-context
RetroAgent variant shows slightly lower diversity than the
single-feedback ablations, suggesting that the two feedback channels may
partially constrain exploration when used jointly
(Table [7](#S4.T7 "Table 7 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).

(a) Impact of the relevance–utility tradeoff coefficient $`\alpha`$ on
RetroAgent (in-context self-reflection).

(b) Impact of the self-reflection objective
weight $`\lambda_{\text{reflect}}`$ on RetroAgent (RL-trained
self-reflection).

Figure 9: Sensitivity of RetroAgent to two key coefficients on the
WebShop test set, reported in terms of task score and success rate
(averaged over three runs).

### 4.9 Training Efficiency

Figure 10: Training time (wall-clock hours) on WebShop. “Time to Match
GRPO” denotes the time required for each RetroAgent variant to reach the
peak performance of the GRPO baseline.

We evaluate training efficiency by comparing RetroAgent with the GRPO
baseline in terms of training time
(Figure [10](#S4.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 4.9 Training Efficiency ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).

Intrinsic feedback accelerates convergence. Although RetroAgent incurs
higher total training cost than GRPO, it reaches GRPO’s peak performance
substantially earlier. The in-context variant matches the GRPO peak at
step 65, while the RL-trained variant does so at step 73
(Figure [8(a)](#S4.F8.sf1 "Figure 8(a) ‣ Figure 8 ‣ 4.7 Effect of Combining Dual Intrinsic Feedback ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),
reducing the time required to reach this performance by $`46\%`$ and
$`32\%`$, respectively. The RL-trained variant converges slightly more
slowly, likely due to the additional optimization of the reflection
objective.

### 4.10 Sensitivity to the Relevance–Utility Tradeoff Coefficient

We examine the relevance–utility tradeoff in memory retrieval for
RetroAgent with in-context self-reflection by varying the coefficient
$`\alpha`$ from $`0.3`$ (utility-oriented) to $`0.7`$
(relevance-oriented). As shown in
Figure [9(a)](#S4.F9.sf1 "Figure 9(a) ‣ Figure 9 ‣ 4.8 Effect of Intrinsic Feedback on Exploration ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
RetroAgent achieves higher WebShop task scores and success rates when
utility is prioritized ($`\alpha=0.3`$). This highlights the importance
of accounting for historical utility, rather than relying solely on
semantic relevance, when retrieving experiential memories.

### 4.11 Sensitivity to the Self-Reflection Objective Weight

We examine the effect of the self-reflection objective
weight $`\lambda_{\text{reflect}}`$ on the final performance of
RetroAgent with RL-trained self-reflection mechanism by varying
$`\lambda_{\text{reflect}}`$ from $`0`$ (self-reflection loss disabled)
to $`1`$. As shown in
Figure [9(b)](#S4.F9.sf2 "Figure 9(b) ‣ Figure 9 ‣ 4.8 Effect of Intrinsic Feedback on Exploration ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
increasing $`\lambda_{\text{reflect}}`$ consistently improves
performance on WebShop, raising the success rate from 75.8% to 82.3% and
the task score from 86.0% to 88.9%.

### 4.12 Generalization Across Model Architectures

|  |  |  |  |  |  |

|  | Success (%) | Score (%) | Success (%) | Success (%) | Success (%) |
| Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (Zero-shot) | $`29.2_{\pm 0.9}`$ | $`0.2_{\pm 0.4}`$ | $`0.1_{\pm 0.2}`$ | $`5.7_{\pm 0.5}`$ | $`7.0_{\pm 0.8}`$ |
| GRPO (Baseline) | $`72.7_{\pm 2.3}`$ | $`78.0_{\pm 2.3}`$ | $`67.6_{\pm 2.8}`$ | $`12.2_{\pm 1.2}`$ | $`42.4_{\pm 2.5}`$ |
| LaMer ([Jiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)) | $`76.0_{\pm 1.8}`$ | \- | $`70.3_{\pm 3.6}`$ | $`15.9_{\pm 2.4}`$ | $`32.0_{\pm 3.4}`$ |
| GiGPO ([Feng et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)) | $`90.9_{\pm 3.6}`$ | $`87.8_{\pm 2.3}`$ | $`77.7_{\pm 3.9}`$ | $`13.5_{\pm 1.2}`$ | $`48.2_{\pm 2.0}`$ |
| RetroAgent (In-Context) | $`\mathbf{93.1_{\pm 1.5}}`$ | $`87.8_{\pm 1.8}`$ | $`71.9_{\pm 3.6}`$ | $`\mathbf{39.1_{\pm 1.3}}`$ | $`52.3_{\pm 1.6}`$ |
| RetroAgent (RL-Trained) | $`91.4_{\pm 1.4}`$ | $`\mathbf{89.5_{\pm 2.1}}`$ | $`\mathbf{80.5_{\pm 2.2}}`$ | $`24.5_{\pm 2.8}`$ | $`\mathbf{59.9_{\pm 3.2}}`$ |

Table 9: Performance of RetroAgent on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across four
agentic benchmarks. All improvements are statistically significant
($`p<0.01`$).

RetroAgent generalizes across model architectures. To assess
architectural generality, we evaluate RetroAgent with
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ([Grattafiori et al., 2024](#bib.bib69)). As shown
in
Table [9](#S4.T9 "Table 9 ‣ 4.12 Generalization Across Model Architectures ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
RetroAgent consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance across all
four tasks. The RL-trained self-reflection variant slightly
underperforms the in-context variant on ALFWorld and Sokoban, likely due
to interference between reflection and decision-making objectives during
joint optimization. Specifically, the auxiliary reflection loss may
weaken the primary policy-gradient signal. We leave improved
multi-objective balancing to future work.

### 4.13 Scaling Across Model Sizes

(a) Task score comparison.

(b) Success rate comparison.

Figure 11: Performance comparison of various methods across different
model scales.

RetroAgent generalizes across model scales. We evaluate RetroAgent on
WebShop using Qwen2.5-Instruct models at 7B and 14B scales. As shown in
Figure [11](#S4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ 4.13 Scaling Across Model Sizes ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
RetroAgent consistently outperforms competitive baselines at both
scales. Scaling from 7B to 14B yields modest gains: task score improves
by $`+0.9\%`$–$`+3.8\%`$, and success rate by $`+1.3\%`$–$`+1.6\%`$.
These results suggest that larger models can better encode and exploit
experiential knowledge, but that model scaling alone provides limited
benefits when the compared models share similar pretraining
distributions and thus similar underlying reasoning capabilities.

### 4.14 Qualitative Analysis

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/compared_trajectories.png)

Figure 12: Qualitative comparison of RetroAgent (in-context
self-reflection) on the WebShop validation set between training step 65
(failed trajectory, left) and training step 150 (successful trajectory,
right). For conciseness, only action tokens and their corresponding
probabilities are shown at each decision step.

We qualitatively examine RetroAgent’s policy evolution by analyzing how
lessons distilled from similar past tasks guide decision-making during
training. We compare an early failed trajectory from RetroAgent with
in-context self-reflection at step 65 and a later successful trajectory
at step 150 on the WebShop validation set. As shown in
Figure [12](#S4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 4.14 Qualitative Analysis ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
at step 65, RetroAgent selects an incorrect item at decision Step 1,
fails to choose the required pink variant, and assigns low token-level
confidence to the correct category “youth.” In contrast, at step 150,
RetroAgent retrieves relevant lessons from memory and selects the
correct item with the required attributes at higher confidence. Full
trajectories are provided in
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Generated Trajectories by RetroAgent ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

## 5 Conclusion

We present RetroAgent, an online RL framework that bridges one-off task
solving and continuous adaptation. Through a hindsight self-reflection
mechanism, RetroAgent generates dual intrinsic feedback:
$`(\textup{\it i})`$ intrinsic numerical feedback that rewards promising
exploration by tracking incremental subtask completion, and
$`(\textup{\it ii})`$ intrinsic language feedback that distills reusable
lessons into a memory buffer. This memory is retrieved via SimUtil-UCB,
which balances relevance, utility, and exploration to leverage prior
experience effectively. By jointly learning from extrinsic task-success
rewards and retrospective dual intrinsic feedback, RetroAgent enables
efficient experiential learning. Experiments across four diverse agentic
tasks show that RetroAgent consistently achieves SOTA performance while
exhibiting strong test-time adaptation and out-of-distribution
generalization. These results suggest that dual intrinsic feedback is a
promising direction for building continuously adaptive agents. Future
work includes developing more effective multi-objective optimization
strategies for jointly training self-reflection and decision-making, and
extending RetroAgent to multi-agent and open-ended settings.

## Ethics Statement

Throughout this research, we have adhered to ethical guidelines that
prioritize privacy, fairness, and the responsible development of
autonomous agents. All experiments are conducted on publicly available
interactive decision-making benchmarks (ALFWorld, WebShop, Sokoban, and
MineSweeper) within fully simulated, sandboxed environments. Our study
does not involve human subjects, personally identifiable information, or
private data, thereby safeguarding user privacy by design. The proposed
intrinsic feedback mechanisms—capability-evolution rewards and
memory-based language feedback—operate entirely within these simulated
environments and do not interact with real users or real-world systems.
All training is performed using open-source base models, ensuring
transparency and reproducibility. These measures were implemented to
minimize potential negative impacts on users and society.

## Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback

We analyze how reflection memory and intrinsic numerical feedback
jointly affect policy optimization. Memory retrieval changes the rollout
distribution by increasing the probability of sampling trajectories with
high reflected potential, while the capability-evolution reward assigns
larger return to such trajectories. Thus, trajectories exhibiting
partial progress can be reinforced even without extrinsic success.

Let $`q_{0}=x`$ be the original prompt and

|     |                                          |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       q_{m}=f_{\mathrm{memory}}(x,\mathcal{M})
       ```                                       |     |

be the memory-augmented prompt. For a fixed behavior policy
$`\pi_{\theta_{\mathrm{old}}}`$, define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Q_{0}(\tau)=p_{\theta_{\mathrm{old}}}(\tau\mid q_{0}),\qquad Q_{m}(\tau)=p_{\theta_{\mathrm{old}}}(\tau\mid q_{m}).
``` |  |

If memory-augmented rollouts are sampled with probability
$`\rho\in[0,1]`$, the rollout distribution is

|     |                                                      |     |      |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       Q_{\rho}(\tau)=(1-\rho)Q_{0}(\tau)+\rho Q_{m}(\tau).
       ```                                                   |     | (14) |

In RetroAgent, $`\rho=1/2`$.

Recall that $`\tau`$ is $`\delta`$-valuable at iteration $`k`$ if

|     |                                             |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \phi_{(x,\tau),k}\geq\Phi_{x}^{(k)}+\delta.
       ```                                          |     |

Let

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p_{k,\delta}^{0}=\Pr_{\tau\sim Q_{0}}\left[\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\right],\qquad p_{k,\delta}^{m}=\Pr_{\tau\sim Q_{m}}\left[\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\right].
``` |  |

Then, under $`Q_{\rho}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p_{k,\delta}^{\rho}=\Pr_{\tau\sim Q_{\rho}}\left[\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\right]=(1-\rho)p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\rho p_{k,\delta}^{m}.
``` |  | (15) |

#### Intrinsic feedback as selective gradient weighting.

At iteration $`k`$, the augmented reward is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau)=R^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tau)+R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau),\qquad R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)=\left[\phi_{(x,\tau),k}-\Phi_{x}^{(k)}\right]_{+}.
``` |  | (16) |

For $`q\in\{q_{0},q_{m}\}`$, define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\theta;q)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau)\right].
``` |  | (17) |

Treating rewards as trajectory-level feedback, the likelihood-ratio
identity gives

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}J_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\theta;q)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\right]`$ |  | (18) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[R^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tau)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\right].`$ |  |  |

Thus, intrinsic feedback adds the gradient component

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
g_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\theta;q)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\right].
``` |  | (19) |

For every $`\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)`$,
$`R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\geq\delta`$, whereas trajectories below the
current baseline receive no intrinsic reward. Hence, the
capability-evolution reward selectively upweights trajectories whose
reflected potential exceeds the current capability threshold.

In practice, returns are replaced by advantages. Let

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau,q)=R_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau)-b_{k}(q),
``` |  | (20) |

where $`b_{k}(q)`$ is an action-independent baseline, such as the GRPO
group mean. Since

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\left[b_{k}(q)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\right]=0,
``` |  |

the policy gradient is equivalently

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nabla_{\theta}J_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\theta;q)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\left[A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau,q)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\right].
``` |  | (21) |

Therefore, even if $`R^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tau)=0`$, a trajectory can
receive positive gradient weight whenever its augmented advantage is
positive. Intrinsic feedback consequently separates failures with
meaningful reflected progress from uninformative failures.

###### Proposition 8 (Intrinsic feedback lower-bounds valuable-trajectory weight).

Fix $`q\in\{q_{0},q_{m}\}`$, and define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p_{k,\delta}(\theta;q)=\Pr_{\tau\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\left[\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\right].
``` |  |

For

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\theta;q)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\right],
``` |  |

we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\theta;q)\geq\delta\,p_{k,\delta}(\theta;q).
``` |  | (22) |

Moreover,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}J_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\theta;q)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\mathbf{1}\{\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}\right]`$ |  | (23) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q)\mathbf{1}\{\tau\notin\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}\right],`$ |  |  |

where the first term assigns at least $`\delta`$ intrinsic return weight
to each $`\delta`$-valuable trajectory.

###### Proof.

For $`\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)`$,
$`R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\geq\delta`$, and otherwise
$`R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\geq 0`$. Hence,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle J_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\theta;q)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\delta\,\mathbf{1}\{\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\delta\,p_{k,\delta}(\theta;q).`$ |  |

The gradient decomposition follows by inserting

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
1=\mathbf{1}\{\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}+\mathbf{1}\{\tau\notin\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}
``` |  |

into the likelihood-ratio gradient. ∎

#### Joint effect.

The mixture objective is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{k}^{\rho}(\theta)=(1-\rho)J_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\theta;q_{0})+\rho J_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\theta;q_{m}).
``` |  | (24) |

Its gradient is

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}J_{k}^{\rho}(\theta)`$ | $`\displaystyle=(1-\rho)\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim Q_{0}}\left[A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau,q_{0})\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q_{0})\right]`$ |  | (25) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\rho\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim Q_{m}}\left[A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau,q_{m})\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q_{m})\right].`$ |  |  |

Thus, memory retrieval affects the sampling distribution, while
intrinsic feedback affects return and advantage weights.

###### Theorem 9 (Joint improvement from memory retrieval and intrinsic reward).

Assume memory augmentation increases the probability of sampling a
$`\delta`$-valuable trajectory by at least $`\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}>0`$:

|     |                                                             |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       p_{k,\delta}^{m}\geq p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}.
       ```                                                          |     | (26) |

Then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p_{k,\delta}^{\rho}\geq p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\rho\Delta_{\mathcal{M}},
``` |  | (27) |

and

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim Q_{\rho}}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\right]\geq\delta\left(p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\rho\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}\right).
``` |  | (28) |

Therefore, relative to base-only sampling, the intrinsic-reward lower
bound improves by at least $`\rho\delta\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}`$. For
$`\rho=1/2`$, the gain is at least $`\delta\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}/2`$.

###### Proof.

By
Equation ([15](#A1.E15 "Equation 15 ‣ Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"))
and
Equation ([26](#A1.E26 "Equation 26 ‣ Theorem 9 (Joint improvement from memory retrieval and intrinsic reward). ‣ Joint effect. ‣ Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle p_{k,\delta}^{\rho}`$ | $`\displaystyle=(1-\rho)p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\rho p_{k,\delta}^{m}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq(1-\rho)p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\rho\left(p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}\right)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\rho\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}.`$ |  |

Since every $`\delta`$-valuable trajectory receives intrinsic reward at
least $`\delta`$, and all intrinsic rewards are nonnegative,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim Q_{\rho}}\left[R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau)\right]\geq\delta p_{k,\delta}^{\rho}\geq\delta\left(p_{k,\delta}^{0}+\rho\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}\right).
``` |  |

∎

#### Internalization through shared parameters.

Because base and memory-augmented prompts share policy parameters, a
high-advantage trajectory sampled under one prompt can increase the
likelihood of related behavior under the other prompt when their score
gradients are aligned.

Let $`\tau^{+}\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)`$ be sampled under
$`q_{a}\in\{q_{0},q_{m}\}`$, and suppose
$`A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau^{+},q_{a})>0`$. A single-sample update is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\theta^{+}=\theta+\eta A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau^{+},q_{a})\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{a}),
``` |  | (29) |

with learning rate $`\eta>0`$. If $`\tau^{+}`$ is extrinsically failed
but valuable, then $`R^{\mathrm{ext}}(\tau^{+})=0`$ and
$`R_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}(\tau^{+})\geq\delta`$, so it may still be
reinforced whenever its augmented advantage is positive.

###### Proposition 10 (Internalization of memory-guided exploration).

Let $`\tau^{+}\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)`$ be sampled under
$`q_{a}\in\{q_{0},q_{m}\}`$, and let $`q_{b}\in\{q_{0},q_{m}\}`$. Under
Equation ([29](#A1.E29 "Equation 29 ‣ Internalization through shared parameters. ‣ Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")),

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\log p_{\theta^{+}}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{b})-\log p_{\theta}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{b})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\eta A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau^{+},q_{a})\left\langle\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{b}),\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{a})\right\rangle`$ |  | (30) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+O(\eta^{2}).`$ |  |  |

Thus, if the inner product is positive, then for sufficiently small
$`\eta`$ the update increases the likelihood of $`\tau^{+}`$ under
$`q_{b}`$ to first order.

###### Proof.

A first-order Taylor expansion gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\log p_{\theta^{+}}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{b})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\log p_{\theta}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{b})+\left\langle\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau^{+}\mid q_{b}),\theta^{+}-\theta\right\rangle+O(\|\theta^{+}-\theta\|_{2}^{2}).`$ |  |

Substituting
Equation ([29](#A1.E29 "Equation 29 ‣ Internalization through shared parameters. ‣ Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"))
proves
Equation ([30](#A1.E30 "Equation 30 ‣ Proposition 10 (Internalization of memory-guided exploration). ‣ Internalization through shared parameters. ‣ Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")).
Since $`A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau^{+},q_{a})>0`$, positive alignment
gives a positive first-order change. ∎

###### Corollary 1 (Expected transfer from memory-augmented to base prompting).

Define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
h_{0}(\tau)=\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q_{0}),\qquad h_{m}(\tau)=\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q_{m}).
``` |  |

Suppose

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim Q_{m}}\left[A_{k}^{\mathrm{aug}}(\tau,q_{m})\left\langle h_{0}(\tau),h_{m}(\tau)\right\rangle\mathbf{1}\{\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}\right]>0.
``` |  | (31) |

Then, for sufficiently small $`\eta`$, a policy-gradient update on
memory-augmented valuable trajectories increases their expected
log-likelihood under the base prompt to first order:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim Q_{m}}\left[\left(\log p_{\theta^{+}}(\tau\mid q_{0})-\log p_{\theta}(\tau\mid q_{0})\right)\mathbf{1}\{\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}\right]>0,`$ |  | (32) |

up to $`O(\eta^{2})`$ terms.

###### Proof.

Apply
Proposition [10](#Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 10 (Internalization of memory-guided exploration). ‣ Internalization through shared parameters. ‣ Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
with $`q_{a}=q_{m}`$ and $`q_{b}=q_{0}`$, multiply by
$`\mathbf{1}\{\tau\in\mathcal{V}_{k,\delta}(x)\}`$, and take expectation
over $`\tau\sim Q_{m}`$. The first-order term is positive by
Equation ([31](#A1.E31 "Equation 31 ‣ Corollary 1 (Expected transfer from memory-augmented to base prompting). ‣ Internalization through shared parameters. ‣ Appendix A Theoretical Analysis – Joint benefit of dual intrinsic feedback ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"));
for sufficiently small $`\eta`$, it dominates the $`O(\eta^{2})`$
remainder. ∎

Overall, memory retrieval shifts sampling toward trajectories with
higher reflected potential, intrinsic numerical feedback upweights
trajectories that exceed the current capability baseline, and shared
parameters allow high-advantage memory-guided behaviors to transfer to
the base prompt when score gradients are aligned. Together, these
mechanisms explain how RetroAgent improves exploration, credit
assignment, and online adaptation under sparse extrinsic rewards.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Hyperparameter | Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct | Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct |  Description |
| Training Configuration |  |  |  |
| Training batch size | 16 | 16 |  Accumulated batch size per update |
| Validation batch size | 128 | 128 |  Batch size for validation |
| Learning rate | $`10^{-6}`$ | $`10^{-6}`$ |  Optimizer learning rate |
| Max prompt length | 16 384 | 16 384 |  Maximum input context length (tokens) |
| Max response length | 2 048 | 2 048 |  Maximum generated response length (tokens) |
| Group size ($`N`$) | 8 | 8 |  Number of rollouts per prompt |
| Total steps | 150 / 300 | 150 / 300 |  Training epochs (150 for ALFWorld and WebShop; 300 for Sokoban and Minesweeper) |
| Evaluation frequency | 5 | 5 |  Epochs between consecutive evaluations |
| Reward and Regularization |  |  |  |
| Extrinsic reward ($`R^{\text{ext}}`$) | $`\{0,\,10\}`$ | $`\{0,\,10\}`$ |  Scalar reward from the environment |
| Intrinsic reward ($`R^{\text{int}}`$) | $`[0,\,1]`$ | $`[0,\,1]`$ |  Capability-evolution intrinsic reward |
| KL coefficient ($`\beta`$) | 0.01 | 0.01 |  KL-divergence regularization weight |
| Discount factor ($`\gamma`$) | 0.95 | 0.95 |  Discount factor for multi-step returns |
| Memory and Sampling |  |  |  |
| Training temperature | 0.4 | 0.4 |  Sampling temperature during rollouts |
| Validation temperature | 0.4 | 0.4 |  Sampling temperature during validation |
| Initial utility score | 0.5 | 0.5 |  Initial utility assigned to each memory entry |
| Utility smoothing ($`\beta_{\text{util}}`$) | 0.05 | 0.05 |  Exponential moving average coefficient for utility updates |
| UCB exploration constant ($`c`$) | 1.0 | 1.0 |  Exploration coefficient in UCB-based retrieval |
| Relevance–utility weight ($`\alpha`$) | 0.7 | 0.7 |  Trade-off coefficient in retrieval scoring |
| Memory-augmented ratio | $`1{:}1`$ | $`1{:}1`$ |  Ratio of memory-augmented to base rollouts |
| Self-Reflection (RL-Trained Variant) |  |  |  |
| Reflection reward ($`R^{\text{reflect}}`$) | $`\{0,\,10\}`$ | $`\{0,\,10\}`$ |  Scalar reward for reflection accuracy |
| Reflection weight ($`\lambda_{\text{reflect}}`$) | 1.0 | 1.0 |  Weight of the self-reflection objective relative to the decision-making objective |
| Evaluation Configuration |  |  |  |
| Evaluation temperature | 0.4 | 0.4 |  Sampling temperature during evaluation |
| Max inference tokens | 2 048 | 2 048 |  Maximum token budget per inference step |

RetroAgent across all environments.

provided in
Table [10](#A2.T10 "Table 10 ‣ Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").
We use sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2.^(§)^(§) §

All experiments were conducted on 4 NVIDIA H200 GPUs.

## Appendix C Superiority of Pairwise Induction over Single Induction

We analyze reflection sequences generated during GRPO training augmented
with either single-trajectory or pairwise-trajectory induction.
Specifically, we sample four sequences per task prompt every 10 training
steps, randomly selecting 800 sequences from this pool. Using GPT-4o as
an external evaluator, we assess two metrics: the alignment between the
agent’s induced subtask completion scores and oracle outcomes (induced
by GPT-4o), and the quality of the agent’s summarized lessons (see
Appendix [E.3](#A5.SS3 "E.3 Prompts for Analyzing the Quality of Intrinsic Feedback ‣ Appendix E Task Prompts ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
for evaluation prompts).

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/single_discrimination_analysis.png)

(a) Single induction.

![Refer to caption](2603.08561v6/pairwise_discrimination_analysis.png)

(b) Pairwise induction.

Figure 13: Comparison of intrinsic discrimination capability for
Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct using single vs. pairwise induction, measured by
subtask completion scores on WebShop.

Figure [13](#A3.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Appendix C Superiority of Pairwise Induction over Single Induction ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback")
compares the ability of single- and pairwise-trajectory induction to
discriminate trajectory quality within the training-free self-reflection
mechanism. For this analysis, we define a successful trajectory as
having a completion score of 1. While both methods correctly assign
minimal scores to “Total Failure” cases, pairwise induction demonstrates
superior discrimination for intermediate outcomes. For example, as shown
in
Figure [13(a)](#A3.F13.sf1 "Figure 13(a) ‣ Figure 13 ‣ Appendix C Superiority of Pairwise Induction over Single Induction ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"),
single induction struggles to distinguish “Early Progress” from “Total
Failure,” resulting in a distribution heavily skewed toward zero for
partial successes. In contrast, pairwise induction
(Figure [13(b)](#A3.F13.sf2 "Figure 13(b) ‣ Figure 13 ‣ Appendix C Superiority of Pairwise Induction over Single Induction ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback"))
effectively recognizes partial progress, assigning distinctly higher
scores to “Early Progress” trajectories and establishing a clear
separation from total failures.

### E.3 Prompts for Analyzing the Quality of Intrinsic Feedback

To assess the fidelity of the intrinsic feedback generated via
self-reflection, we employ GPT-4o ([OpenAI et al., 2024](#bib.bib67)) as
an external judge. Our evaluation focuses on two key components: the
accuracy of the induced subtask completion scores (intrinsic rewards)
and the quality of the summarized lessons (intrinsic feedback).

To verify the accuracy of the subtask completion scores, we utilize the
prompt detailed in
Section [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Prompt Templates for In-Context Self-Reflection ‣ Appendix E Task Prompts ‣ RetroAgent: From Solving to Evolving via Retrospective Dual Intrinsic Feedback").

agent’s trajectories, we use the prompt presented below.
````
